                        <section class="pmc-journal-banner text-center line-height-none" aria-label="Journal banner"><img src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/banners/logo-scirep.jpg" alt="Scientific Reports logo" usemap="#pmc-banner-imagemap" width="500" height="75"><map name="pmc-banner-imagemap"><area alt="Link to Scientific Reports" title="Link to Scientific Reports" shape="default" href="http://www.nature.com/srep" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"></map></section><article lang="en"><section aria-label="Article citation and metadata"><section class="pmc-layout__citation font-secondary font-xs"><div>
<div class="display-inline-block"><button type="button" class="cursor-pointer text-no-underline bg-transparent border-0 padding-0 text-left margin-0 text-normal text-primary" aria-controls="journal_context_menu">Sci Rep</button></div>. 2021 Jan 25;11:2150. doi: <a href="https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-021-81232-5" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">10.1038/s41598-021-81232-5</a>

<hgroup><h1>Estimating multiple latencies in the auditory system from auditory steady-state responses on a single EEG channel</h1></hgroup><div class="cg p">
<a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Wang%20L"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id1"><span class="name western">Lei Wang</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id1">
<h3><span class="name western">Lei Wang</span></h3>
<div class="p">
<sup>1</sup>Department of Biophysics, Radboud University, Nijmegen, 6525 AJ The Netherlands </div>
<div class="p">
<sup>2</sup>Donders Centre for Neuroscience, Radboud University, Nijmegen, 6525 AJ The Netherlands </div>
<div class="p">Find articles by <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Wang%20L"[Author]' class="usa-link"><span class="name western">Lei Wang</span></a>
</div>
</div>
<sup>1,</sup><sup>2,</sup><sup>✉</sup>, <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Noordanus%20E"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id2"><span class="name western">Elisabeth Noordanus</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id2">
<h3><span class="name western">Elisabeth Noordanus</span></h3>
<div class="p">
<sup>1</sup>Department of Biophysics, Radboud University, Nijmegen, 6525 AJ The Netherlands </div>
<div class="p">
<sup>2</sup>Donders Centre for Neuroscience, Radboud University, Nijmegen, 6525 AJ The Netherlands </div>
<div class="p">Find articles by <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Noordanus%20E"[Author]' class="usa-link"><span class="name western">Elisabeth Noordanus</span></a>
</div>
</div>
<sup>1,</sup><sup>2</sup>, <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="van%20Opstal%20AJ"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id3"><span class="name western">A John van Opstal</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id3">
<h3><span class="name western">A John van Opstal</span></h3>
<div class="p">
<sup>1</sup>Department of Biophysics, Radboud University, Nijmegen, 6525 AJ The Netherlands </div>
<div class="p">

<div class="p">Find articles by <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="van%20Opstal%20AJ"[Author]' class="usa-link"><span class="name western">A John van Opstal</span></a>
</div>
</div>
<sup>1,</sup><sup>2</sup>

<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2020 Oct 20; Accepted 2021 Jan 5; Collection date 2021.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">

If material is not included in the article's Creative Commons licence and your intended use is not permitted by statutory regulation or exceeds the permitted use, you will need to obtain permission directly from the copyright holder. To view a copy of this licence, visit <a href="http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/</a>.</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC7835249  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33495484/" class="usa-link">33495484</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="Abs1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p id="Par1">The latency of the auditory steady-state response (ASSR) may provide valuable information regarding the integrity of the auditory system, as it could potentially reveal the presence of multiple intracerebral sources. To estimate multiple latencies from high-order ASSRs, we propose a novel two-stage procedure that consists of a nonparametric estimation method, called apparent latency from phase coherence (ALPC), followed by a heuristic sequential forward selection algorithm (SFS). Compared with existing methods, ALPC-SFS requires few prior assumptions, and is straightforward to implement for higher-order nonlinear responses to multi-cosine sound complexes with their initial phases set to zero. It systematically evaluates the nonlinear components of the ASSRs by estimating multiple latencies, automatically identifies involved ASSR components, and reports a latency consistency index. To verify the proposed method, we performed simulations for several scenarios: two nonlinear subsystems with different or overlapping outputs. We compared the results from our method with predictions from existing, parametric methods. We also recorded the EEG from ten normal-hearing adults by bilaterally presenting superimposed tones with four frequencies that evoke a unique set of ASSRs. From these ASSRs, two major latencies were found to be stable across subjects on repeated measurement days. The two latencies are dominated by low-frequency (LF) (near 40 Hz, at around 41–52 ms) and high-frequency (HF) (&gt; 80 Hz, at around 21–27 ms) ASSR components. The frontal-central brain region showed longer latencies on LF components, but shorter latencies on HF components, when compared with temporal-lobe regions. In conclusion, the proposed nonparametric ALPC-SFS method, applied to zero-phase, multi-cosine sound complexes is more suitable for evaluating embedded nonlinear systems underlying ASSRs than existing methods. It may therefore be a promising objective measure for hearing performance and auditory cortex (dys)function.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Subject terms:</strong> Biomedical engineering, Auditory system</p></section></section><section id="Sec1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p id="Par23">Auditory steady-state responses (ASSRs) are stable brain oscillations that are locked to the frequencies present in the periodic envelope of acoustic stimuli<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a>,<a href="#CR2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR2">2</a></sup>. Studies have indicated that ASSRs may be generated at different levels within the auditory pathway, ranging from as early as the cochlear nerve and subcortical sources<sup><a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">3</a>,<a href="#CR4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR4">4</a></sup>, to the neocortex<sup><a href="#CR5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR5">5</a>–<a href="#CR8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR8">8</a></sup>. Because of their reproducibility, and involuntary nature, ASSRs have been considered a valid objective biomarker for auditory system disorders that feature abnormal sound processing, as well as for evaluating primary and non-primary auditory cortex function<sup><a href="#CR9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR9">9</a>,<a href="#CR10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR10">10</a></sup>. Its presence could relate to basic bottom-up sound processing<sup><a href="#CR11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR11">11</a>,<a href="#CR12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR12">12</a></sup>, as well as to more complex cognitive skills, such as auditory learning<sup><a href="#CR13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR13">13</a></sup>, selective attention<sup><a href="#CR14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR14">14</a></sup>, speech and music perception<sup><a href="#CR7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR7">7</a></sup>, mental disorders<sup><a href="#CR15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR15">15</a></sup>, or illnesses such as tinnitus<sup><a href="#CR9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR9">9</a>,<a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">16</a></sup>. Furthermore, since ASSRs may contain signatures of the neural processing from auditory periphery to cortex, the electrically evoked ASSR from a cochlear implant (CI) could provide an objective measure for the responsiveness of different regions of the auditory pathway of hearing-impaired CI-users<sup><a href="#CR17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR17">17</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="Par24">It should be noted that ASSRs are due to nonlinear mechanisms in the auditory system (see<sup><a href="#CR18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR18">18</a></sup>, for a review). At the earliest stages in the auditory pathway, the nonlinear character of neural sound encoding has been evaluated from auditory nerve recordings. A stimulus consisting of multiple pure tones (the carrier frequencies) was shown to result in an envelope spectrum that could be characterized by a unique series of 2nd-order nonlinear difference frequencies (or ‘beats’) between any pair of carrier frequencies, which all showed up in the auditory nerve response. This indicates that these 2nd-order nonlinear distortions already arise at, or before, the auditory nerve<sup><a href="#CR19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR19">19</a></sup>. In the ascending auditory pathway, such harmonic complexes might also evoke higher-order nonlinear components, which in turn could give rise to additional ASSRs. In particular, when two pure tones with frequencies <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq23"><math id="M2"><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq24"><math id="M4"><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math></span>, are passed through a nonlinear system with system order <em>R</em>, a series of combination tones is produced at the output, characterized by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq25"><math id="M6"><mrow><mi>n</mi><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>±</mo><mi>m</mi><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></math></span> (&gt; 0), with <em>n</em> and <em>m</em> positive integers, such that <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq26"><math id="M8"><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>m</mi><mo>≤</mo><mi>R</mi></mrow></math></span><sup><a href="#CR16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR16">16</a>,<a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">20</a></sup>. So far, few studies have systematically evaluated such higher-order nonlinearities in the EEG responses of the human auditory system.</p>
<p id="Par25">Apart from these nonlinear distortion components, also the latency at which they occur in the EEG provides information of the underlying neural mechanisms, and could reveal the contribution from different intracerebral sources<sup><a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a>,<a href="#CR7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR7">7</a>,<a href="#CR21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR21">21</a></sup>. Although the auditory system consists of both ascending and descending pathways<sup><a href="#CR5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR5">5</a></sup>, the ASSRs recorded at the scalp are assumed to be mainly due to the ascending system. Therefore, the longer the latency, the higher in the auditory system its generators<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="Par26">A steady-state response is characterized by its amplitude and phase. The phase (with certain ambiguity) can be used to extract a so-called ‘apparent latency’ (the slope of the phase versus frequency plot) introduced by Regan (1966). In auditory physiology, the apparent latency is described as ‘group delay’<sup><a href="#CR22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR22">22</a></sup> and has been used to measure the latency of the motion of the basilar membrane, the cochlear microphonic, otoacoustic emissions, and the discharge of auditory nerve fibers, although the actual relation of apparent latency to physiological delay is not clear<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a></sup>. Specifically, apparent latency, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq27"><math id="M10"><mi>τ</mi></math></span>, is derived from the relation between the response phase, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq28"><math id="M12"><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mi>ϕ</mi></mrow></math></span>, and frequency: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq29"><math id="M14"><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mi>ϕ</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><mi>f</mi><mo>·</mo><mi>τ</mi></mrow></math></span>), and is used as an indirect method to estimate the neural response latency for steady-state stimuli, when a direct measurement is not feasible<sup><a href="#CR22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR22">22</a></sup>. This method, however, suffers from the inherent <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq30"><math id="M16"><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow></math></span> phase ambiguity from the so-called wrap-around effect<sup><a href="#CR23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR23">23</a></sup>, and is only suitable for analyzing low-order ASSR components (e.g., envelope frequencies) when the initial phases are known (so that phase lags can be computed). The more recently-developed multi-spectral phase-coherence (MSPC) method<sup><a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">20</a></sup>; or similarly, the cross-spectral coherence (CSC) method<sup><a href="#CR24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR24">24</a></sup>, resolves the phase-ambiguity problem, and allows evaluation of higher-order nonlinear response components. However, the MSPC method relies on prior assumptions regarding the underlying nonlinear systems: knowledge about the system orders is required for evaluating the expected nonlinear interactions, from which response latencies can be estimated. Therefore, the MSPC method is difficult to implement when the number of underlying systems and their orders (and thus, their nonlinear interactions) are unknown. Moreover, when using multiple carrier frequencies, there is an explosion of the number of potential nonlinear interactions: for given nonlinear systems and multi-tone sinusoidal inputs, a recursive method<sup><a href="#CR25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR25">25</a></sup> can yield the series of output frequencies. The number of output frequencies is a steeply increasing function of the system order <em>R</em> and the number of input frequencies, <em>I</em>, and its accurate computation is non-trivial because of potentially overlapping distortion products<sup><a href="#CR26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR26">26</a></sup>; see also Appendix C in Supplemental Material).</p>
<p id="Par27">Thus, the current state-of-the-art methods are limited in estimating multiple latencies for different nonlinear systems from the ASSRs. However, as indicated above, several studies have suggested that ASSR sources can be located at multiple cortical and subcortical regions<sup><a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">3</a>,<a href="#CR6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR6">6</a></sup>, which are likely associated with different latencies. For example, the two-component (brainstem-cortex) model suggests that a cortical generator will dominate low-frequency distortion products (around 45 Hz), whereas a brainstem generator would dominate the higher modulation frequencies (around 90 Hz)<sup><a href="#CR2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR2">2</a>,<a href="#CR27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR27">27</a>,<a href="#CR28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR28">28</a></sup>. As the measured EEG will typically contain the components from these multiple generators, a more general hybrid nonlinear model would read:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ1"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M18" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><mi>y</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>M</mi></munderover><mrow><munderover><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>r</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><msub><mi>R</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></munderover><mrow><msub><mi>ψ</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">mr</mi></mrow></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><msubsup><mi>x</mi><mi>m</mi><mi>r</mi></msubsup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>m</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">1</td>
</tr></table>
<p>in which the unknown parameters are the number of subsystems <em>M</em>, the subsystem order <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq31"><math id="M20"><msub><mi>R</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></math></span>, the subsystem latencies <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq32"><math id="M22"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></math></span>, as well as the potentially frequency-dependent gains, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq33"><math id="M24"><msub><mi>ψ</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">mr</mi></mrow></msub></math></span>. Note that, in general, it will not be possible to uniquely identify each nonlinear system, when the underlying nonlinearities and their inputs are unknown. Yet, for certain cases, system-specific information can be obtained from the ASSRs. Figure <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1</a> illustrates a potential simple scenario of such nonlinear models. The EEG is considered to reflect the (linear) sum of the outputs from multiple nonlinear subsystems (here taken, for simplicity, as homogeneous nonlinearities of different orders, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq34"><math id="M26"><msub><mi>R</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></math></span>, and each at a different latency, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq35"><math id="M28"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></math></span>). The output distortion frequencies of these subsystems will differ when they receive non-overlapping inputs (e.g., systems <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq36"><math id="M30"><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq37"><math id="M32"><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math></span>). In this case, the latencies of both subsystems can be accurately estimated. In contrast, the output distortion products could be largely overlapping when the systems receive identical inputs, as in <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq38"><math id="M34"><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq39"><math id="M36"><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>M</mi></msub></math></span>. In such a case, it will be much harder to estimate the underlying latencies. In this paper, we simulated both cases.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig1"><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/df87/7835249/5410736d7812/41598_2021_81232_Fig1_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO2" height="269" width="668" alt="Figure 1"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Simplified scenario of M nonlinear subsystems that underlie the measured EEG response. The input signal <em>X</em>(<em>t</em>) contains <em>I</em> frequencies. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq40"><math id="M38"><mrow><mi>B</mi><msub><mi>P</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> represent different band-pass filters, so that the subsystems (with order <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq41"><math id="M40"><msub><mi>R</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></math></span> and latency <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq42"><math id="M42"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></math></span>) may be driven by the same or by different input frequencies. The EEG contains the linear sum of all subsystem outputs <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq43"><math id="M44"><mrow><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>m</mi></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>, with <em>N</em> nonlinear distortion components <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq44"><math id="M46"><msub><mi>f</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></msub></math></span> (ASSRs).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par28">There are currently no suitable methods to deal with such models, and the the commonly used apparent latency (or group delay) method cannot be readily aplied. The apparent latency method requires computation of phase lags for a group of nearby frequencies, which is difficult for higher-order ASSRs, due to (1) the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq45"><math id="M48"><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow></math></span> wrapping problem (i.e., how many cycles have passed?), (2) the nonlinear distortion of phases for higher-order ASSRs (which could introduce a bias within <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq46"><math id="M50"><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow></math></span>, see Appendix C in Supplemental Material), and (3) higher-order ASSRs often cover a large frequency range. Thus, the assumption that a group of target frequencies share a common latency does not apply anymore. As described earlier, the SPC method may partly account for the first two problems through a parametric approach, which then hinges on the assumption of the underlying nonlinear system orders (and their corresponding nonlinear interactions). It then estimates a latency that follows the apparent latency rule (i.e., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq47"><math id="M52"><mrow><mi>τ</mi><mo>=</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mi>ϕ</mi><mo stretchy="false">/</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><mi>f</mi></mrow></math></span>). Here, we applied the MSPC method as an extension of the classical group delay method for higher-order ASSRs. However, the method assumes a common latency for all nonlinear response components with the same system order (e.g., all 2nd order responses), which might not be suitable for the ASSR analysis when the EEG signals arise from multiple subsystems (as illustrated in Fig. <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1</a>).</p>
<p id="Par29">We here propose and test an alternative, nonparametric, semi-heuristic method that requires few prior assumptions regarding the underlying EEG sources, and aims to objectively estimate multiple potential latencies from the ASSRs. We named our method ‘apparent latency from phase coherence’ (ALPC-SFS), which is applied to a specifically designed stimulus complex, and implements a heuristic sequential forward selection (SFS) algorithm<sup><a href="#CR29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR29">29</a></sup> to identify the components of each subsystem. Our method can estimate multiple latencies corresponding to more than one underlying source by using only one EEG experiment. Our ALPC-SFS method does not have to make prior assumptions about the number of underlying systems, their input frequencies, or their system orders. To test our method, we performed simulations for several scenarios, and recorded EEG responses from ten normal-hearing participants to superimposed tones that evoked high-order ASSRs during passive listening.</p>
<p id="Par30">We also compared our ALPC-SFS method with the existing MSPC method, for both the simulation data and the EEG recordings. Both methods can be viewed as extensions of the classical group-delay method for higher-order ASSRs, as both follow the apparent latency rule. They will achieve an identical latency estimate if only one common latency exists. However, when multiple potential latencies underlie the EEG, our ALPS-SFS method will outperform the MSPC method (and the group delay method), achieving correct latencies for each subsystem, and identifying the associated frequencies that drive each subsystem (see Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig3"><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/df87/7835249/a37daa31a7bf/41598_2021_81232_Fig3_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO12" height="766" width="668" alt="Figure 3"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Pipeline for estimating the apparent latency from the EEG signal, using the parametric MSPC (left) or non-parametric ALPC-SFS (right) approach. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq120"><math id="M206"><msubsup><mi>φ</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">in</mi></mrow></msubsup></math></span> represents the initial phase of each frequency component in the stimuli (input), and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq121"><math id="M208"><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>n</mi></msub></msub></math></span> represents the initial phase of each ASSR frequency component (output). By using multi-cosine stimuli with their initial phases set to zero, the ALPC-SFS method (right) uses time compensation by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq122"><math id="M210"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></math></span> to avoid the more complex phase-compensation procedure of the MSPC method (left; see Appendix A in Supplemental Material), which computes phase lags of the ASSRs. Moreover, the former can identify potentially multiple latencies, [<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq123"><math id="M212"><mrow><msub><mi>τ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>τ</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mo>⋯</mo></mrow></math></span>], instead of only one (biased) dominant latency, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq124"><math id="M214"><msub><mover accent="true"><mi>τ</mi><mo stretchy="false">^</mo></mover><mn>1</mn></msub></math></span>.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="Sec2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Methods</h2>
<p id="Par31">To estimate multiple latencies from recorded ASSRs, we developed a two-stage procedure that consists of the nonparametric ALPC estimation method, followed by the heuristic SFS algorithm. The ALPC method can apply phase compensation on stimuli with arbitrary initial phases (as used in<sup><a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">20</a></sup>), or it can use a much simpler time compensation (TC) procedure for a special stimulus complex, in which the initial phases of all stimulus frequencies, and thus their nonlinear distortion products, are set to zero. Note that the former requires prior information regarding the underlying nonlinear orders of systems and the corresponding nonlinear interaction (see Appendix A in Supplemental Material). In contrast, ALPC with TC requires no such information, and thus can be viewed as a nonparametric method (see the right-hand side of Fig. <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3</a>). We combined ALPC with SFS and TC to identify multiple latencies from the ASSRs.</p>
<section id="Sec3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Problem description</h3>
<p id="Par32">The EEG is considered as a time-series signal, <em>S</em>, containing a number of distortion frequency components <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq48"><math id="M54"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>N</mi></msub></msub><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></math></span>, with corresponding angular frequencies <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq49"><math id="M56"><mrow><msub><mi>ω</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><msub><mi>f</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></mrow></math></span>, which are the outputs of a nonlinear system. Assuming that <em>N</em> frequency components of them have a common, unknown, delay <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq50"><math id="M58"><mi>τ</mi></math></span> relative to their initial phase <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq51"><math id="M60"><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></math></span> at t = 0 (potentially having a common generator), we compute the current phase angle <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq52"><math id="M62"><msub><mi>α</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></math></span> of each component by taking the Fourier transform (FT), where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq53"><math id="M64"><mrow><msub><mi>α</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>. They follow:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ2"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M66" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><msub><mi>α</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>τ</mi><msub><mi>ω</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>ε</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mrow></mrow><msub><mi>α</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>τ</mi><msub><mi>ω</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><msub><mi>n</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>ε</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mrow></mrow><mo>⋮</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mrow></mrow><msub><mi>α</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>N</mi></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>τ</mi><msub><mi>ω</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>N</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>N</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>N</mi></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>ε</mi><mi>N</mi></msub></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">2</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq54"><math id="M68"><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math></span> are unknown integers, i.e., the number of cycles that a component is delayed, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq55"><math id="M70"><msub><mi>ε</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math></span> is the phase error (PE), caused by random noise.</p>
<p id="Par33">To estimate the common latency, we minimize the mean-squared phase error across <em>N</em> frequencies<sup><a href="#CR30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR30">30</a></sup>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ3"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M72" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><munder><mo movablelimits="false">min</mo><mi>τ</mi></munder><mfenced close=")" open="("><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mi>N</mi></mfrac><munderover><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></munderover><mrow><mspace width="0.277778em"></mspace><mo>mod</mo><mspace width="0.277778em"></mspace><msup><mrow><mfenced close=")" open="("><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>α</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>τ</mi><msub><mi>ω</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow></mfrac></mfenced></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">3</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq56"><math id="M74"><mrow><mspace width="0.277778em"></mspace><mo>mod</mo><mspace width="0.277778em"></mspace><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo>·</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> is the modulus between 0 and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq57"><math id="M76"><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow></math></span>. If the nonlinear system order, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq58"><math id="M78"><mrow><mi>R</mi><mo>≤</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span>, the initial phases are straightforward to measure from the input stimulus by extracting the phases from the envelope of the input signal. However, when the system’s order is unknown, and higher than three, this method will fail, because the unknown <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq59"><math id="M80"><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></math></span> are endowed with the wrap-around problem (i.e., unknown <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq60"><math id="M82"><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math></span>).</p></section><section id="Sec4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">General framework of ALPC</h3>
<p id="Par34">First, to avoid that a discontinuous ‘jump’ (from 0 to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq61"><math id="M84"><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow></math></span>) would affect the averaging in (<a href="#Equ3" class="usa-link">3</a>), we represent the components of (<a href="#Equ2" class="usa-link">2</a>) by Euler’s formula according to: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq62"><math id="M86"><mrow><mo>exp</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>j</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>α</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>τ</mi><msub><mi>ω</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mo>exp</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>ε</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span> or, equivalently, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq63"><math id="M88"><mrow><mrow><mo>exp</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>j</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>α</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>τ</mi><msub><mi>ω</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mo>exp</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>j</mi><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>ε</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq64"><math id="M90"><msub><mi>ε</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math></span> is a vectorized phase error (PE) and its length <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq65"><math id="M92"><mrow><mfenced close="|" open="|"><msub><mi>ε</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mfenced><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mn>2</mn><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>. We then minimize the cost, defined by the mean length of PE (MPE) across N frequencies, to estimate <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq66"><math id="M94"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub></math></span>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ4"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M96" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><munder><mo movablelimits="false">min</mo><mi>τ</mi></munder><mfenced close=")" open="("><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mi>N</mi></mfrac><munderover><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>N</mi></munderover><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mrow><mo>exp</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>j</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>α</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>τ</mi><msub><mi>ω</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>-</mo><mrow><mo>exp</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>j</mi><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></mrow></mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">4</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq67"><math id="M98"><mfenced close="|" open="|"><mrow><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mo>·</mo><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace></mrow></mfenced></math></span> is the length of a complex vector. Using the estimated <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq68"><math id="M100"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub></math></span>, we can compute <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq69"><math id="M102"><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math></span> and the absolute phase lag of each frequency by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq70"><math id="M104"><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>α</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></mrow></math></span> (equal to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq71"><math id="M106"><mrow><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><msub><mi>ω</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>ε</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>). Plotting the absolute phase lags for each frequency component as a function of frequency should yield a line with a slope <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq72"><math id="M108"><msub><mi>d</mi><mi>ϕ</mi></msub></math></span>, where the relationship between <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq73"><math id="M110"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq74"><math id="M112"><msub><mi>d</mi><mi>ϕ</mi></msub></math></span> follows the apparent latency rule<sup><a href="#CR1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR1">1</a></sup>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ5"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M114" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><msub><mi>d</mi><mi>ϕ</mi></msub><mrow><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi></mrow></mfrac><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">5</td>
</tr></table>
<p>In (<a href="#Equ4" class="usa-link">4</a>), we estimate <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq75"><math id="M116"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub></math></span> by searching within a period <em>T</em> of signal <em>S</em>, where <em>T</em> is the smallest common multiple of the periods of frequency components <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq76"><math id="M118"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>.</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>N</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></math></span>. Then, the solutions for the latency are given by a set <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq77"><math id="M120"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mi>τ</mi><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>τ</mi><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><mi>m</mi><mi>T</mi><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></math></span>, with <em>m</em> an arbitrary integer. In a particular application, a <em>prior</em> for the possible range of latency values can be imposed to constrain a unique value for <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq78"><math id="M122"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub></math></span>.</p>
<section id="Sec5"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Time compensation (TC) for stimuli with fixed initial phases</h4>
<p id="Par35">To avoid the need for computing the unknown <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq79"><math id="M124"><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></math></span> when using stimuli with arbitrary initial phases (which constitutes ‘phase compensation’, see Appendix A in Supplemental Material), we here propose to use a more practical stimulus complex: a multi-cosine stimulus, in which all initial phases are set to zero at t = 0. With such a stimulus complex it is straightforward to apply time compensation (TC) for an arbitrary ongoing interval (starting at t &gt; 0) in the following way (see Fig. <a href="#Fig2" class="usa-link">2</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig2"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=7835249_41598_2021_81232_Fig2_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/df87/7835249/7b158267fe47/41598_2021_81232_Fig2_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO7" height="300" width="797" alt="Figure 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Illustration of time compensation (TC) on a single-channel EEG. The stimulus complex consisted of a superposition of four cosine pure tones, at 461, 500, 504 and 537 Hz, starting at t = 0 with initial phase 0. Here, the six distortion frequencies (i.e., the 2nd order system products) in the top panel at [4, 33, 37, 39, 43, 76] Hz, are shown and time-scaled by a factor 0.1, for illustrative purposes. Note that all six distortions also start with zero phase at t = 0 (because of the good property of cosine; see Appendix C in Supplemental Material). The EEG response to the stimulus complex is shown in the bottom panel (1st trial on EEG electrode P8, subj#1) and has been down-sampled to 200 Hz for easy visualization. The first <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq82"><math id="M126"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></math></span> = 0.3 s of the EEG signal is excluded from analysis because of the initial non-stationary ERP components (i.e., non-ASSR).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par36">For a given multi-cosine input stimulus, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq83"><math id="M128"><mrow><mi>s</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>I</mi></munderover><mrow><mo>cos</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>ω</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">in</mi></mrow></msubsup><mi>t</mi><mo>+</mo><msubsup><mi>φ</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">in</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>, with all initial phases <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq84"><math id="M130"><mrow><msubsup><mi>φ</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">in</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></math></span> at <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq85"><math id="M132"><mrow><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></math></span>, the ongoing stimulus interval, starting at <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq86"><math id="M134"><mrow><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, is denoted as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq87"><math id="M136"><mrow><mi>s</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mi>t</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>. The delayed EEG signal (the ASSR) <em>d</em>(<em>t</em>) is considered to be the output of an unknown nonlinear system with an unknown latency, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq88"><math id="M138"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub></math></span>, i.e., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq89"><math id="M140"><mrow><mi>d</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mi>F</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>s</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq90"><math id="M142"><mrow><mi>F</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo>·</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> represents the unknown nonlinear mapping. The EEG epoch of interest will hence follow:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ6"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M144" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mi>d</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mi>t</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mo>=</mo><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mi>F</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>s</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mi>t</mi></msub><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mover><mo>=</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi></mover><mi>F</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>s</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>p</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mrow></mrow><mspace width="1em"></mspace><mi mathvariant="normal">with</mi><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>p</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo>-</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">6</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Therefore, from the ASSR we can estimate the pseudo-latency <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq91"><math id="M146"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></math></span> between <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq92"><math id="M148"><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mi>t</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq93"><math id="M150"><mrow><mi>s</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>t</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>. Since <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq94"><math id="M152"><mrow><msubsup><mi>φ</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">in</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></math></span>, the corresponding initial phases <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq95"><math id="M154"><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></math></span> of all high-order responses are also zero (i.e., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq96"><math id="M156"><mrow><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>=</mo><munderover><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>r</mi></munderover><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><msubsup><mi>φ</mi><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">in</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>≡</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></math></span>, see Appendix A and C in Supplemental Material). Moreover, it can be shown that for zero-phase sine inputs, all even-order difference interactions (e.g., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq97"><math id="M158"><mrow><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq98"><math id="M160"><mrow><mn>2</mn><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>m</mi></msub></mrow></math></span>) will yield the same initial phase of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq99"><math id="M162"><mrow><mi>π</mi><mo stretchy="false">/</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span> rad, so that the method can also be applied to these components (after changing the initial phase to <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq100"><math id="M164"><mrow><mi>π</mi><mo stretchy="false">/</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></math></span>; see Appendix C). Therefore, from (<a href="#Equ4" class="usa-link">4</a>) and (<a href="#Equ6" class="usa-link">6</a>), we can estimate the pseudo latency <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq101"><math id="M166"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>p</mi></msub></math></span>. Finally, we apply TC to obtain the actual latency:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ7"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M168" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>e</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>τ</mi><mi>p</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mi>t</mi></msub><mo>.</mo></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">7</td>
</tr></table>
<p>In our study, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq102"><math id="M170"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></math></span>= 0.3 s, in order to exclude the non-stationary event-related potentials (ERP) in the EEG after stimulus onset (see Fig. <a href="#Fig2" class="usa-link">2</a>). The device jitter (resulting to be &lt; 0.5 ms) involved a small trigger jitter of the EEG recording system, and a fixed travel time of the acoustic signal through the silicon tube to the ears. Thus, the estimated latency had around 1-ms system error, which was deemed acceptable for the estimated ASSR latencies.</p></section><section id="Sec6"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Latency consistency index (LCI)</h4>
<p id="Par37">Considering that the estimated latency may vary due to additive noise on the nonstationary signals, we quantified its consistency over <em>K</em> time epochs. We defined the latency consistency index (LCI) for the <em>i</em>th output frequency <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq104"><math id="M172"><msub><mi>f</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></math></span> as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq105"><math id="M174"><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mi>τ</mi><mi>i</mi></msubsup></math></span>.</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ8"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M176" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mi>τ</mi><mi>i</mi></msubsup></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>=</mo><mfenced close="|" open="|"><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mi>K</mi></mfrac><munderover><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>K</mi></munderover><mo>exp</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>j</mi><msub><mi>ω</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><msub><mi>τ</mi><mrow><mi>e</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow></mrow></mtd><mtd columnalign="left"><mrow><mo>=</mo><mfenced close="|" open="|"><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mi>K</mi></mfrac><munderover><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>K</mi></munderover><mo>exp</mo><msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>j</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>α</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mi>k</mi></msub></mfenced></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">8</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq106"><math id="M178"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mrow><mi>e</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> is the estimated latency for frequency component <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq107"><math id="M180"><msub><mi>f</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></math></span> on the <em>k</em>th epoch, and it follows <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq109"><math id="M182"><mrow><msub><mi>ω</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><msub><mi>τ</mi><mrow><mi>e</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><msub><mi>n</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>α</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></mrow></math></span> from (<a href="#Equ2" class="usa-link">2</a>), assuming Gaussian noise with zero mean. The LCI is hence mathematically equivalent to the phase-coupling strength used in MSPC<sup><a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">20</a></sup>, which shows that the higher the phase-coupling strength between inputs and outputs, the more stable the estimated latency. We selected the duration of each time epoch (without overlap) as multiples of the periods of the ASSR frequencies (e.g., integer seconds, given integer ASSR frequencies) so that the initial phase <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq110"><math id="M184"><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub></math></span> remains the same across epochs. In this case, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq111"><math id="M186"><mrow><msub><mi>φ</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mo>≡</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></math></span> when the cosine carriers have their initial phases set to zero. Thus, the LCI no longer relies on the stimulus frequencies, and reads:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ9"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M188" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mi>τ</mi><mi>i</mi></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mfenced close="|" open="|"><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mi>K</mi></mfrac><munderover><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mi>K</mi></munderover><mo>exp</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>j</mi><msub><mrow><msub><mi>α</mi><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">Σ</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">9</td>
</tr></table>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq112"><math id="M190"><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mi>τ</mi><mi>i</mi></msubsup></math></span> can vary between 0 and 1. If the latency is perfectly stable over time epochs, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq113"><math id="M192"><mrow><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mi>τ</mi><mi>i</mi></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span>. Otherwise, if no stable latency exists, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq114"><math id="M194"><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mi>τ</mi><mi>i</mi></msubsup></math></span> is statistically indistinguishable from zero. Therefore, LCI can be used to determine if an ASSR component can be used to estimate latencies. The theoretical threshold for significance is <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq115"><math id="M196"><mrow><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mi>τ</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">sig</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mfrac><mn>3</mn><mi>K</mi></mfrac></msqrt></mrow></math></span><sup><a href="#CR31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR31">31</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="Par38">The null hypothesis is that the phase of an ASSR component is randomly distributed in the interval <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq116"><math id="M198"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mspace width="0.166667em"></mspace><mn>2</mn><mi>π</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>. If <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq117"><math id="M200"><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mi>τ</mi><mi>i</mi></msubsup></math></span> exceeds <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq118"><math id="M202"><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mi>τ</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">sig</mi></mrow></msubsup></math></span>, the null hypothesis is rejected, and the ASSR component is considered to be significant. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq119"><math id="M204"><msubsup><mi>C</mi><mi>τ</mi><mrow><mi mathvariant="italic">sig</mi></mrow></msubsup></math></span> approximates the 95% confidence limit, as verified by Monte Carlo simulation<sup><a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">20</a></sup>. In our analysis, only significant ASSR components were considered for estimating latencies.</p></section></section><section id="Sec7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">ALPC with sequential forward selection (SFS)</h3>
<p id="Par39">A particular set of ASSR components may have a common latency that leads to a low cost of the MPE in (<a href="#Equ4" class="usa-link">4</a>). In addition, the set of ASSR components may contain several subsets that correspond to different latencies, which would be indicative for different underlying sources. To automatically identify a frequency subset from all significant ASSR components that belong to a common latency, we applied the heuristic SFS algorithm. SFS is commonly used as an alternative to a greedy algorithm, to reduce computational complexity. SFS starts with a frequency (or frequency pair), and then sequentially adds another frequency (from the remaining set) that results in the smallest increase of the cost (i.e., MPE in a range of [0–2]). The details of the SFS algorithm are described in<sup><a href="#CR29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR29">29</a>,<a href="#CR32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR32">32</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="Par40">The termination rule of SFS should be carefully chosen, depending on the data at hand. Here, we used the following procedure. When starting from a single frequency, SFS continued as long as the following two criteria were met: (i) the increase of the MPE of the current step remained &lt; 0.1, (ii) the current MPE &lt; 0.5. Otherwise, SFS terminates. If SFS started from a pair of frequencies, it continued when the following three criteria were met: (i) the difference of estimated latencies between the current and the previous step &lt; 5 ms, (ii) the step increase of MPE &lt; 0.1, (iii) the current MPE &lt; 0.5. Otherwise, SFS terminates. These criteria may be further tuned for the particular EEG data set.</p>
<p id="Par41">Figure <a href="#Fig3" class="usa-link">3</a> summarizes the pipeline for estimating the apparent latency from the EEG by the MSPC and ALPC-SFS methods, respectively. Depending on the initial phases of the carriers (either arbitrary, or all zero), one can use either a parametric (the left-hand side, MSPC, or group delay) or a non-parametric (the right-hand side; ALPC-SFS) approach to estimate the underlying latency. For the parametric approach, phase compensation (see Appendix A in Supplemental Material) can be used to estimate the unknown initial phases of the ASSRs, which are fed to the MSPC algorithm to estimate the dominant latency. Note that the phase compensation method requires prior assumptions regarding the nonlinear system. For the non-parametric approach as proposed in the present paper, ALPC with SFS can estimate multiple potential latencies, by selecting the involved ASSR frequencies for each subsystem.</p></section></section><section id="Sec8"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">EEG experiments</h2>
<section id="Sec9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Experimental design and stimuli</h3>
<p id="Par42">We performed EEG recording experiments on 10 normal-hearing adults (1 female, age 29 ± 13 y) (hearing thresholds &lt; 20 dB HL). Each subject was measured twice, using the same stimuli on different days (i.e., &gt; 48 h interval). The stimulus at the left ear was a superposition of two tones of 461 Hz and 500 Hz at 81 dB SPL, and two tones of 504 Hz and 537 Hz at 56 dB SPL. The stimuli at the right ear consisted of the reverse pattern: two loud tones of 504 Hz and 537 Hz at 81 dB SPL, and two weaker tones of 461 Hz and 500 Hz at 56 dB SPL. The stimuli were fed into both ears simultaneously. The four frequencies were chosen such that the difference frequencies and higher-order distortions were all unique (see Fig. <a href="#MOESM1" class="usa-link">A2</a> in the Supplemental Material).</p>
<p id="Par43">In the EEG experiments described here, all input tones were sines with their initial phases at t = 0 set to zero. In Appendix C of Supplemental Material, we demonstrate that such carriers will yield irregular phase-shifts for all higher-order distortion products, except for the even-order difference interactions (e.g., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq125"><math id="M216"><mrow><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>-</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></math></span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq126"><math id="M218"><mrow><mn>2</mn><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn><msub><mi>f</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></math></span>). The initial phase of these even-order distortion products will remain the same. Thus, when only even-order difference interactions in the ASSRs are considered (as in this study), APLC-SFS with TC can also be applied for zero-phase sine stimuli.</p>
<p id="Par44">In each trial, stimuli with a duration of 12.3 s were played after a silent break with a random duration between 2 and 3 s. It has been reported that the primary auditory cortex responds within 20 ms to stimulus changes and integrates stimulus features over a period of about 200 ms, which is the pre-steady-state phasic response phase<sup><a href="#CR33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR33">33</a></sup>. Therefore, the first 300 ms of the EEG of each trial was excluded from analysis to avoid the contributions from phasic event-related potentials (i.e., non-ASSRs). We expect the ASSR latencies to remain constant during the subsequent 12 s of the ASSR stage, which will allow for an accurate estimate of the neighboring SNR (see section E). We repeated 100 trials while recording the scalp EEG with a 64-channel cap with Ag/AgCl electrodes, with the listener sitting in an anechoic chamber, watching a silent video. The EEG signals were digitized at a sampling rate of 2000 Hz by a Refa amplifier system (TMSi, Twente Medical Systems International B.V., the Netherlands), and stored for offline analysis. At the start of each recording session, the impedances of all EEG electrodes were carefully set to values below 10 <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq127"><math id="M220"><mrow><mi>k</mi><mi mathvariant="normal">Ω</mi></mrow></math></span> to ensure good contacts between electrodes and scalp. During the session, these values were regularly checked, and adjusted when deemed necessary. Stimuli were generated by TDT (Tucker-Davis Technologies, USA) system 3 hardware, and presented through ER3C insert earphones (Etymotic Research, Elk GroveVillage, IL), which were connected to the participant’s ears via 30 cm-long plastic tubes and foam earplugs.</p>
<p id="Par45">The experiments were approved by the ethics committee at Radboud University and performed in accordance with the human experiment guidelines and regulations of Radboud university. We confirm that informed consent was obtained from all subjects.</p></section><section id="Sec10"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Preprocessing of EEG</h3>
<p id="Par46">We applied a three-stage preprocessing protocol on the raw EEG recordings, after first removing the three EEG electrodes above the eyes (FP1, FPz, and FP2) because they were contaminated by eye-blinks and eye-movement artifacts. First, the raw EEG signals were re-referenced to a common average reference (CAR) to reduce the noise from the grounding electrode<sup><a href="#CR34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR34">34</a></sup>. Compared with re-reference in relation to a certain EEG electrode that may not be ‘neutral’ to ASSRs, the CAR montage will keep the phases of ASSRs unchanged on most EEG electrodes. Second, each EEG channel was filtered with a zero-phase shift filter<sup><a href="#CR35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR35">35</a></sup> that consisted of a 10th-order Butterworth high-pass filter with a cutoff frequency of 1 Hz, and a notch filter at 50 Hz to remove the line noise. Third, to exclude severely contaminated EEG trials from further analysis (mainly by EMG artifacts, which may cause false detections or decreased SNRs of ASSR components), we developed a method to automatically exclude bad EEG trials. To that end, we computed the mean and the variance of the peak-to-peak amplitude range as indicators on each EEG trial. We excluded EEG trials whose indicators were positive outliers on 100 trials, i.e., samples &gt; Q3 + 1.5 * IQR. (Interquartile range (IQR) = upper quartile (Q3) − lower quartile (Q1)). In this way, up to 5% of all trials were automatically excluded from further analysis.</p></section><section id="Sec11"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Epoch filtering by phase lock value (PLV)</h3>
<p id="Par47">We tested whether excluding poorly phase-locked epochs would improve the accuracy of the phase estimates, and latencies, for the frequencies of interest. To that end, we divided each trial into twelve one-second epochs, and computed their phase-lock value (PLV <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq128"><math id="M222"><mrow><mo>∈</mo><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></math></span>) to each frequency of interest<sup><a href="#CR36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR36">36</a></sup>. PLV = 0 represents no phase locking between the frequency band of EEG and the frequency of interest, while PLV = 1 represents the complete phase locking. Epochs with a PLV value below a empirically selected threshold, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq129"><math id="M224"><mi>θ</mi></math></span> (taken as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq130"><math id="M226"><mi>θ</mi></math></span> = 0, 0.4, 0.6, or 0.8) were excluded. Finally, we computed the phase values on the remaining epochs by using two different averaging methods: AVG EEG, and AVG phase (described below).</p></section><section id="Sec12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Phase extraction of EEG</h3>
<p id="Par48">The accuracy of the extracted phases of the frequency components is important for estimating latency. Previous studies often average the EEG signals across a number of trials to reduce the background noise, so that ASSRs have a higher SNR<sup><a href="#CR37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR37">37</a></sup>. Here, we employed two different methods, ‘AVG EEG’ and ‘AVG phase’ to extract the phase of a target frequency. The ‘AVG EEG’ method averages filtered EEG signals of all trials and subsequently computes the phases of the average EEG. The phase values from this method tend to be dominated by epochs with strong ASSRs (i.e., with large amplitudes). Instead, the ‘AVG phase’ method averages the extracted phases from all EEG epochs with a duration that is an integer multiple of the signal period (1 sec in this study), without considering amplitude information (as used in<sup><a href="#CR20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR20">20</a></sup>). For this method, all EEG epochs thus contributed with equal weight to the estimate. For comparison, we used both methods to extract the phases on each EEG channel independently.</p></section><section id="Sec13"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">SNR on target frequencies</h3>
<p id="Par49">It is difficult to separate the signals generated by ASSRs from background noise in the EEG on a single-trial basis. However, after averaging the EEG across a number of trials (100 trials in this study), the power of phase-locked frequencies is enhanced, whereas the power of the background EEG is suppressed due to random phases. To quantify the SNR of each ASSR frequency component, we defined the neighboring SNR as<sup><a href="#CR3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR3">3</a></sup>:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="Equ10"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="M228" display="block"><mrow><mtable><mtr><mtd columnalign="right"><mrow><mi>S</mi><mi>N</mi><mi>R</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>t</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>log</mo><mn>10</mn><mfenced close=")" open="("><mfrac><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>t</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mi>N</mi></mfrac><munderover><mo movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mi>i</mi><mi>N</mi></munderover><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mfrac></mfenced></mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">10</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq131"><math id="M230"><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>t</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span> is the power of the target frequency bin <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq132"><math id="M232"><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></math></span>. The <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq133"><math id="M234"><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math></span> is a neighboring frequency bin within a small range (± 0.5 Hz) of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq134"><math id="M236"><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>t</mi></msub></math></span>. For a 12 s EEG trial, the width of a frequency bin is 1/12 Hz and <em>N</em> = 12. Compared with the frequency spectrum of the EEG, the neighboring SNR is robust to the pink noise present in EEG signals (see Fig. <a href="#Fig9" class="usa-link">9</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="Fig9"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 9.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=7835249_41598_2021_81232_Fig9_HTML.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/df87/7835249/e7ee3f55ad4c/41598_2021_81232_Fig9_HTML.jpg" loading="lazy" id="MO23" height="306" width="668" alt="Figure 9"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/Fig9/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Grand average SNRs (n = 10 subjects on recording days 1 and 2, top panel) and grand averaging scalp topography (n = 20 measurements over two days, bottom panel) on ten major ASSR components indicated by arrows. The third-largest SNR (out of 61 channels) on each integer frequency was averaged across 10 subjects measured on day 1 or day 2. To calculate the grand average of the scalp topography, the SNR of each measurement was normalized between 0 and 1 to enable a direct comparison of each subject’s contribution.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="Par50">Note that the defined neighboring SNR is not an unbiased estimate of real SNR. In fact, the expected neighboring SNR <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq135"><math id="M238"><mrow><mi>E</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>S</mi><mi>N</mi><mi>R</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>t</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mi>S</mi><mi>N</mi><mi>R</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></math></span><sup><a href="#CR38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR38">38</a></sup>. More specifically, the defined neighboring SNR follows a F distribution with (2, 2<em>N</em>) degrees of freedom (when the target frequency bin contains no signal), where <em>N</em> is the number of neighboring frequency bins. Given a type-I error (e.g., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq136"><math id="M240"><mi>α</mi></math></span> = 0.05) from the F test, the significance threshold <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq137"><math id="M242"><mi>θ</mi></math></span> is a function of the number of neighboring frequency bins<sup><a href="#CR39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR39">39</a></sup>, i.e., <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq138"><math id="M244"><mi>θ</mi></math></span> = finv(1 − <em>α</em>, 2, 2<em>N</em>) by using the MATLAB (2018a) function ‘finv’. For <em>N</em> = 12, the corresponding thresholds for a significance level <em>p</em> of 0.05 and 0.01 are 3.401 (5.318 dB) and 5.614 (7.492 dB), respectively.</p></section></section><section id="Sec14"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results</h2>
<section id="Sec15"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Simulation results</h3>
<p id="Par51">To illustrate the potential of ALPC with SFS in combination with zero-phase cosine stimuli, we simulated different models and scenarios (cf. Fig. <a href="#Fig1" class="usa-link">1</a>). In these examples, two parallel nonlinear sub-systems with different delays could have different, or (partially) shared inputs.</p>
<section id="Sec16"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Example 1: Two pure second-order subsystems with non-overlapping inputs</h4>
<p id="Par52">Figure <a href="#Fig4" class="usa-link">4</a> shows the simulation results of our ALPC-SFS algorithm for the simple example of Eq. (<a href="#Equ11" class="usa-link">11</a>). The total input signal, <em>x</em>(<em>t</em>), consisted of five frequency components: (17, 21, 27, 41, 49) Hz, where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq140"><math id="M246"><msub><mi>x</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math></span> = (17, 21, 27) Hz were fed to a pure second-order subsystem <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq141"><math id="M248"><msub><mi>y</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math></span>, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq142"><math id="M250"><msub><mi>x</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math></span> = (41, 49) Hz to a similar subsystem <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq143"><math id="M252"><msub><mi>y</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math></span>. In this model, system <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq144"><math id="M254"><msub><mi>y</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math></span> generated the distortion products <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq145"><math id="M256"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mn>2</mn><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>±</mo><msub><mi>f</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>34</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>38</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>42</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>44</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>48</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>54</mn><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> Hz, and system <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq146"><math id="M258"><msub><mi>y</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math></span> yielded the non-overlapping components <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq147"><math id="M260"><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mn>8</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>82</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>90</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>98</mn><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></math></span> Hz. Subsystems <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq148"><math id="M262"><msub><mi>y</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math></span> and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq149"><math id="M264"><msub><mi>y</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math></span> had different latencies <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq150"><math id="M266"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math></span> = 51 ms and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq151"><math id="M268"><msub><mi>τ</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math></span> = 21 ms, respectively. <em>Y</em> is the combined output signal, (representing the EEG) with additive Gaussian white noise (WGN), <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq152"><math id="M270"><mrow><mi>ε</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></math></span>.
